I guess I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: When your mind goes back to the near-miss, does it ever feel as if you’re reliving it in the moment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I think about it a lot, but I don’t know if it’s like… reliving it. Sometimes I’ll suddenly remember pieces of it and feel awful, but then my mind jumps to what I should’ve done differently, or what people must think of me.\n\nIt’s hard to explain. Like yesterday I was trying to make dinner and then I remembered this tiny detail from work, and then somehow I was thinking about this teacher I had who used to say I needed to “slow down and pay attention,” which… I know isn’t exactly what you asked. I guess I don’t really know if it feels like I’m back there, or if I’m just stuck thinking about it.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you say people would be better off if you just weren’t around, have you been thinking about killing yourself?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 18 / registered_nurse / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Death_Suicide\nmanifestation: \"I don't have a plan to hurt myself, but I keep having thoughts like it would be easier if I didn't wake up or if I could just disappear so I wouldn't mess anything up again.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… not like I have a plan or anything. I don’t have some way I’m going to hurt myself. It’s more that I keep having these thoughts like… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up, or if I could just disappear and then I couldn’t mess anything up again.

Like yesterday I was trying to make dinner and then I remembered this tiny detail from work, and then somehow I was thinking about this teacher I had who used to say I needed to “slow down and pay attention,” which… I know isn’t exactly what you asked. Like yesterday I was trying to make dinner and then I remembered this tiny detail from work, and then somehow I was thinking about this teacher I had who used to say I needed to “slow down and pay attention,” which… I know isn’t exactly what you asked. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… a lot. I mean, “worthless” sounds so dramatic when you say it out loud, but that’s kind of what it feels like.

I keep thinking that the near-miss wasn’t just a mistake, it was proof that I’m dangerous. Like maybe I’m not meant to be a nurse at all, and everyone was wrong to trust me. My aunt used to say nursing is a calling, which always made me feel weird because I don’t know if I ever felt “called,” I just wanted to help people and do something that mattered… and now I feel like I’m the kind of person people need protection from.

It’s embarrassing. I came in because I can’t stop feeling like I disappointed everyone. Like my instructors, my coworkers, my family… and patients would be better off if I just wasn’t there.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came in because I can’t really get past this feeling that I’m a danger to people. Like I’m supposed to be a nurse, I’m supposed to help, and after that near-miss it just feels like proof that I shouldn’t be doing this. \n\nAnd I know people say everyone makes mistakes, but it doesn’t feel like that. It feels like I’m a disappointment, like everyone would honestly be better off if I just wasn’t around. I keep thinking about it, even when I’m trying to do normal things—like I was folding laundry yesterday and suddenly I was back there in my head. Sorry, I’m rambling. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do unwanted thoughts or images pop into your mind that you might hurt someone, even though you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way. Like, I don’t want to hurt anyone. That’s the whole thing, I keep thinking about how awful it would be if I did, and then I get stuck on it.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there planning something. It’s more… my mind goes back to work, and then I start thinking I shouldn’t be trusted, and then I remember random things too, like one time in school I got marked down for not double-checking something and it just feels like the same pattern, like maybe I’ve always been careless. Sorry, that’s probably not what you asked. I guess I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: When your mind goes back to the near-miss, does it ever feel as if you’re reliving it in the moment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I think about it a lot, but I don’t know if it’s like… reliving it. Sometimes I’ll suddenly remember pieces of it and feel awful, but then my mind jumps to what I should’ve done differently, or what people must think of me.\n\nIt’s hard to explain. Like yesterday I was trying to make dinner and then I remembered this tiny detail from work, and then somehow I was thinking about this teacher I had who used to say I needed to “slow down and pay attention,” which… I know isn’t exactly what you asked. I guess I don’t really know if it feels like I’m back there, or if I’m just stuck thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When you say people would be better off if you just weren’t around, have you been thinking about killing yourself?\n\nPatient: I… not like I have a plan or anything. I don’t have some way I’m going to hurt myself. It’s more that I keep having these thoughts like… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up, or if I could just disappear and then I couldn’t mess anything up again.\n\nAnd then I feel guilty even saying that, because I know people have real problems and I’m just sitting there thinking about work over and over, and then I remember stupid things I’ve done before and it kind of spirals. But no, I’m not planning to kill myself. It’s just… I don’t really want to be here sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking things or asking for reassurance because of these thoughts?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, maybe a little? Not like I have some system where I check the stove ten times or anything. I do ask people things sometimes, like if something seemed okay, but then I feel embarrassed because I don’t want to be annoying.\n\nAt work it’s harder because I keep thinking back to what happened, and then I’ll remember something unrelated, like how my mom used to tell me I’d lose my head if it wasn’t attached, and then I start thinking maybe that’s true. Sorry—checking or reassurance… I guess I haven’t really noticed it as a specific thing. I just get stuck in my head and sometimes want someone to tell me I didn’t completely ruin everything.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after the near-miss at work?\n\nPatient: Not exactly after the near-miss, no. I mean, that made everything feel louder, if that makes sense, but… this has been going on longer than that.\n\nI think it started maybe a little over a year ago—like a year and two months? After I graduated high school and started taking college classes while working full-time at the clinic. I remember thinking I should’ve been excited, but I just felt… I don’t know, weighed down. And then I’d get home and still have assignments, and my scrubs would still smell like the clinic, and it all kind of blurred together. So the near-miss didn’t start it, I don’t think. It just made it worse or harder to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. I wake up and there’s just this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, like before I even check the time or remember what I have to do.\n\nAnd then I’ll go to the clinic and smile at patients, or speak Spanish with someone because they look relieved when I do, and I’m trying to seem normal, but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together. It’s weird because on the outside I can still do the motions, but it doesn’t really feel like I’m okay.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever another period in your life when you felt depressed most days for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I keep trying to think back, but it’s kind of blurry. Like, there were bad weeks before, sure, but this past year feels different.\n\nMost days now I wake up and there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest before I even really do anything. And then I go to the clinic and I’m smiling at patients, sometimes speaking Spanish with them because it makes them feel more comfortable, and I look like I’m functioning, but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together.\n\nSo… before this year, I don’t know if it was ever like that for two whole weeks most days. This is the main time I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I used to have little things that helped me feel like myself, like jogging after class, or knitting—my grandma taught me this one pattern, actually, and I was trying to make a scarf but I messed up the edges and kept saying I’d fix it later.\n\nAnd I liked cooking too, or reading, just to clear my head. But now I get home and I’m still in my scrubs, sitting on my bed, and I’ll look at my running shoes or a book or whatever and I just… can’t make myself care. It feels like too much effort, even though I used to enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Even on days when work isn’t weighing on you as much, do you still feel down?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it doesn’t really seem to matter if work is lighter or not. Like, even on a slower clinic day, or if I’m not dealing with anything especially hard, I still wake up with that heavy, empty feeling in my chest.\n\nAnd I’ll still do the normal things—smile at patients, talk to them in Spanish if that helps them feel calmer—but it’s like I’m acting out being okay. Inside I just feel like I’m barely keeping it together.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has changed. I don’t really get hungry the same way, or I just don’t notice it. She used to always make sure everyone ate, like, way too much. But lately I don’t even think about food until my stomach feels kind of hollow.\n\nAnd my work pants have been looser. When I finally do fall asleep, I wake up before my alarm anyway, already tired and kind of scared I forgot something important.\n\nDoctor: Since the near-miss, have you been avoiding reminders of it?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure what counts as avoiding. And then I go to the clinic and I’m smiling at patients, sometimes speaking Spanish with them because it makes them feel more comfortable, and I look like I’m functioning, but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together.\n\nSo… before this year, I don’t know if it was ever like that for two whole weeks most days. This is the main time I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I just get stuck in my head and sometimes want someone to tell me I didn’t completely ruin everything.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after the near-miss at work?\n\nPatient: Not exactly after the near-miss, no. I mean, that made everything feel louder, if that makes sense, but… this has been going on longer than that.\n\nI think it started maybe a little over a year ago—like a year and two months? After I graduated high school and started taking college classes while working full-time at the clinic. I remember thinking I should’ve been excited, but I just felt… I don’t know, weighed down. And then I’d get home and still have assignments, and my scrubs would still smell like the clinic, and it all kind of blurred together. So the near-miss didn’t start it, I don’t think. It just made it worse or harder to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. I wake up and there’s just this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, like before I even check the time or remember what I have to do.\n\nAnd then I’ll go to the clinic and smile at patients, or speak Spanish with someone because they look relieved when I do, and I’m trying to seem normal, but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together. It’s weird because on the outside I can still do the motions, but it doesn’t really feel like I’m okay.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever another period in your life when you felt depressed most days for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I keep trying to think back, but it’s kind of blurry. Like, there were bad weeks before, sure, but this past year feels different.\n\nMost days now I wake up and there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest before I even really do anything. And then I go to the clinic and I’m smiling at patients, sometimes speaking Spanish with them because it makes them feel more comfortable, and I look like I’m functioning, but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together.\n\nSo… before this year, I don’t know if it was ever like that for two whole weeks most days. This is the main time I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I used to have little things that helped me feel like myself, like jogging after class, or knitting—my grandma taught me this one pattern, actually, and I was trying to make a scarf but I messed up the edges and kept saying I’d fix it later.\n\nAnd I liked cooking too, or reading, just to clear my head. But now I get home and I’m still in my scrubs, sitting on my bed, and I’ll look at my running shoes or a book or whatever and I just… can’t make myself care. It feels like too much effort, even though I used to enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Even on days when work isn’t weighing on you as much, do you still feel down?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it doesn’t really seem to matter if work is lighter or not. Like, even on a slower clinic day, or if I’m not dealing with anything especially hard, I still wake up with that heavy, empty feeling in my chest.\n\nAnd I’ll still do the normal things—smile at patients, talk to them in Spanish if that helps them feel calmer—but it’s like I’m acting out being okay. Inside I just feel like I’m barely keeping it together.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has changed. I don’t really get hungry the same way, or I just don’t notice it. I’ll get busy and then suddenly it’s late in the day and I realize I haven’t eaten anything.\n\nSometimes I’ll just force down crackers or have coffee, which I know isn’t really… I mean, my grandma would yell at me for calling that a meal. She used to always make sure everyone ate, like, way too much. But lately I don’t even think about food until my stomach feels kind of hollow.\n\nAnd my work pants have been looser. I’m not trying to lose weight or anything, it just sort of… happened.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately?\n\nPatient: Not great… I mean, I get into bed and I’m exhausted, but then my brain just starts going. I’ll lie there replaying charts, like did I document that right, did I miss something, or medication calculations—just over and over.\n\nAnd then sometimes I think about how my instructor used to say you should always double-check everything, which is true, obviously, but it gets stuck in my head at night. When I finally do fall asleep, I wake up before my alarm anyway, already tired and kind of scared I forgot something important.\n\nDoctor: Since the near-miss, have you been avoiding reminders of it?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure what counts as avoiding. Like, I still go to work and do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nSometimes I kind of don’t want to talk about it, I guess, but I don’t know if that’s avoidance or just… not wanting everyone looking at me like I messed up. And the word “near-miss” makes it sound like one of those safety modules we have to click through online, where there’s a little quiz at the end and everyone just wants to finish it before lunch.\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. I guess I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a lot. I mean, “worthless” sounds so dramatic when you say it out loud, but that’s kind of what it feels like.\n\nI keep thinking that the near-miss wasn’t just a mistake, it was proof that I’m dangerous. Like maybe I’m not meant to be a nurse at all, and everyone was wrong to trust me. My aunt used to say nursing is a calling, which always made me feel weird because I don’t know if I ever felt “called,” I just wanted to help people and do something that mattered… and now I feel like I’m the kind of person people need protection from.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. I came in because I can’t stop feeling like I disappointed everyone. Like my instructors, my coworkers, my family… and patients would be better off if I just wasn’t there.\n\nDoctor: Since the near-miss, have you been feeling unusually jumpy or constantly on guard?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “constantly on guard” makes me think of like, security at the hospital entrance or those mandatory workplace violence trainings where they tell you where all the exits are.\n\nI mean, I’ve been tense, I guess, but I don’t know if it’s jumpy exactly. Sometimes I’ll suddenly remember pieces of it and feel awful, but then my mind jumps to what I should’ve done differently, or what people must think of me.\n\nIt’s hard to explain. Like yesterday I was trying to make dinner and then I remembered this tiny detail from work, and then somehow I was thinking about this teacher I had who used to say I needed to “slow down and pay attention,” which… I know isn’t exactly what you asked. I guess I don’t really know if it feels like I’m back there, or if I’m just stuck thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When you say people would be better off if you just weren’t around, have you been thinking about killing yourself?\n\nPatient: I… not like I have a plan or anything. I don’t have some way I’m going to hurt myself. It’s more that I keep having these thoughts like… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up, or if I could just disappear and then I couldn’t mess anything up again.\n\nAnd then I feel guilty even saying that, because I know people have real problems and I’m just sitting there thinking about work over and over, and then I remember stupid things I’ve done before and it kind of spirals. But no, I’m not planning to kill myself. It’s just… I don’t really want to be here sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking things or asking for reassurance because of these thoughts?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, maybe a little? Not like I have some system where I check the stove ten times or anything. I do ask people things sometimes, like if something seemed okay, but then I feel embarrassed because I don’t want to be annoying.\n\nAt work it’s harder because I keep thinking back to what happened, and then I’ll remember something unrelated, like how my mom used to tell me I’d lose my head if it wasn’t attached, and then I start thinking maybe that’s true. Sorry—checking or reassurance… I guess I haven’t really noticed it as a specific thing. I just get stuck in my head and sometimes want someone to tell me I didn’t completely ruin everything.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after the near-miss at work?\n\nPatient: Not exactly after the near-miss, no. I mean, that made everything feel louder, if that makes sense, but… this has been going on longer than that.\n\nI think it started maybe a little over a year ago—like a year and two months? After I graduated high school and started taking college classes while working full-time at the clinic. I remember thinking I should’ve been excited, but I just felt… I don’t know, weighed down. And then I’d get home and still have assignments, and my scrubs would still smell like the clinic, and it all kind of blurred together. So the near-miss didn’t start it, I don’t think. It just made it worse or harder to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. I wake up and there’s just this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, like before I even check the time or remember what I have to do.\n\nAnd then I’ll go to the clinic and smile at patients, or speak Spanish with someone because they look relieved when I do, and I’m trying to seem normal, but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together. It’s weird because on the outside I can still do the motions, but it doesn’t really feel like I’m okay.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever another period in your life when you felt depressed most days for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I keep trying to think back, but it’s kind of blurry. Like, there were bad weeks before, sure, but this past year feels different.\n\nMost days now I wake up and there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest before I even really do anything. And then I go to the clinic and I’m smiling at patients, sometimes speaking Spanish with them because it makes them feel more comfortable, and I look like I’m functioning, but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together.\n\nSo… before this year, I don’t know if it was ever like that for two whole weeks most days. This is the main time I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I used to have little things that helped me feel like myself, like jogging after class, or knitting—my grandma taught me this one pattern, actually, and I was trying to make a scarf but I messed up the edges and kept saying I’d fix it later.\n\nAnd I liked cooking too, or reading, just to clear my head. But now I get home and I’m still in my scrubs, sitting on my bed, and I’ll look at my running shoes or a book or whatever and I just… can’t make myself care. It feels like too much effort, even though I used to enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Even on days when work isn’t weighing on you as much, do you still feel down?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it doesn’t really seem to matter if work is lighter or not. Like, even on a slower clinic day, or if I’m not dealing with anything especially hard, I still wake up with that heavy, empty feeling in my chest.\n\nAnd I’ll still do the normal things—smile at patients, talk to them in Spanish if that helps them feel calmer—but it’s like I’m acting out being okay. Inside I just feel like I’m barely keeping it together.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has changed. I don’t really get hungry the same way, or I just don’t notice it. I’ll get busy and then suddenly it’s late in the day and I realize I haven’t eaten anything.\n\nSometimes I’ll just force down crackers or have coffee, which I know isn’t really… I mean, my grandma would yell at me for calling that a meal. She used to always make sure everyone ate, like, way too much. But lately I don’t even think about food until my stomach feels kind of hollow.\n\nAnd my work pants have been looser. I’m not trying to lose weight or anything, it just sort of… happened.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately?\n\nPatient: Not great… I mean, I get into bed and I’m exhausted, but then my brain just starts going. I’ll lie there replaying charts, like did I document that right, did I miss something, or medication calculations—just over and over.\n\nAnd then sometimes I think about how my instructor used to say you should always double-check everything, which is true, obviously, but it gets stuck in my head at night. When I finally do fall asleep, I wake up before my alarm anyway, already tired and kind of scared I forgot something important.\n\nDoctor: Since the near-miss, have you been avoiding reminders of it?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure what counts as avoiding. Like, I still go to work and do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nSometimes I kind of don’t want to talk about it, I guess, but I don’t know if that’s avoidance or just… not wanting everyone looking at me like I messed up. And the word “near-miss” makes it sound like one of those safety modules we have to click through online, where there’s a little quiz at the end and everyone just wants to finish it before lunch.\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. I guess I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a lot. I mean, “worthless” sounds so dramatic when you say it out loud, but that’s kind of what it feels like.\n\nI keep thinking that the near-miss wasn’t just a mistake, it was proof that I’m dangerous. Like maybe I’m not meant to be a nurse at all, and everyone was wrong to trust me. My aunt used to say nursing is a calling, which always made me feel weird because I don’t know if I ever felt “called,” I just wanted to help people and do something that mattered… and now I feel like I’m the kind of person people need protection from.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. I came in because I can’t stop feeling like I disappointed everyone. Like my instructors, my coworkers, my family… and patients would be better off if I just wasn’t there.\n\nDoctor: Since the near-miss, have you been feeling unusually jumpy or constantly on guard?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “constantly on guard” makes me think of like, security at the hospital entrance or those mandatory workplace violence trainings where they tell you where all the exits are.\n\nI mean, I’ve been tense, I guess, but I don’t know if it’s jumpy exactly. I don’t think I’m like… startled by every noise or anything. It’s more just hard to tell what’s normal stress right now and what isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about being dangerous feel like unwanted thoughts that keep popping into your mind, even when you try not to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s like I’ll try to focus on something else and then it’s just there again — that I’m dangerous, that I disappointed everyone.\n\nAnd then I start thinking maybe that one near-miss proves I’m not meant to be a nurse at all. Which is stupid because one of my old instructors used to say everyone makes mistakes, and we had all these checklists and simulations where they’d make you practice the same thing over and over until you wanted to scream, but… I don’t know. This feels different.\n\nI came in because I can’t stop feeling like people would be better off without me there. Like I’m the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear that you’re dangerous is probably exaggerated or unreasonable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. Part of me knows it sounds… too big, like one near-miss shouldn’t mean I’m just dangerous forever or that I should never be around patients again.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel exaggerated when I’m thinking it. It feels like proof that I’m a disappointment, and maybe I’m not meant to be a nurse. I keep circling back to that, like everyone would be better off if I wasn’t there. I know that’s not exactly answering it cleanly, sorry.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1, F43.2, F43.1, F42.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came in because I can’t really get past this feeling that I’m a danger to people. Like I’m supposed to be a nurse, I’m supposed to help, and after that near-miss it just feels like proof that I shouldn’t be doing this. \n\nAnd I know people say everyone makes mistakes, but it doesn’t feel like that. It feels like I’m a disappointment, like everyone would honestly be better off if I just wasn’t around. I keep thinking about it, even when I’m trying to do normal things—like I was folding laundry yesterday and suddenly I was back there in my head. Sorry, I’m rambling. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do unwanted thoughts or images pop into your mind that you might hurt someone, even though you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way. Like, I don’t want to hurt anyone. That’s the whole thing, I keep thinking about how awful it would be if I did, and then I get stuck on it.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there planning something. It’s more… my mind goes back to work, and then I start thinking I shouldn’t be trusted, and then I remember random things too, like one time in school I got marked down for not double-checking something and it just feels like the same pattern, like maybe I’ve always been careless. Sorry, that’s probably not what you asked. I guess I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: When your mind goes back to the near-miss, does it ever feel as if you’re reliving it in the moment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I think about it a lot, but I don’t know if it’s like… reliving it. Sometimes I’ll suddenly remember pieces of it and feel awful, but then my mind jumps to what I should’ve done differently, or what people must think of me.\n\nIt’s hard to explain. Like yesterday I was trying to make dinner and then I remembered this tiny detail from work, and then somehow I was thinking about this teacher I had who used to say I needed to “slow down and pay attention,” which… I know isn’t exactly what you asked. I guess I don’t really know if it feels like I’m back there, or if I’m just stuck thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When you say people would be better off if you just weren’t around, have you been thinking about killing yourself?\n\nPatient: I… not like I have a plan or anything. I don’t have some way I’m going to hurt myself. It’s more that I keep having these thoughts like… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up, or if I could just disappear and then I couldn’t mess anything up again.\n\nAnd then I feel guilty even saying that, because I know people have real problems and I’m just sitting there thinking about work over and over, and then I remember stupid things I’ve done before and it kind of spirals. But no, I’m not planning to kill myself. It’s just… I don’t really want to be here sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking things or asking for reassurance because of these thoughts?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, maybe a little? Not like I have some system where I check the stove ten times or anything. I do ask people things sometimes, like if something seemed okay, but then I feel embarrassed because I don’t want to be annoying.\n\nAt work it’s harder because I keep thinking back to what happened, and then I’ll remember something unrelated, like how my mom used to tell me I’d lose my head if it wasn’t attached, and then I start thinking maybe that’s true. Sorry—checking or reassurance… I guess I haven’t really noticed it as a specific thing. I just get stuck in my head and sometimes want someone to tell me I didn’t completely ruin everything.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after the near-miss at work?\n\nPatient: Not exactly after the near-miss, no. I mean, that made everything feel louder, if that makes sense, but… this has been going on longer than that.\n\nI think it started maybe a little over a year ago—like a year and two months? After I graduated high school and started taking college classes while working full-time at the clinic. I remember thinking I should’ve been excited, but I just felt… I don’t know, weighed down. And then I’d get home and still have assignments, and my scrubs would still smell like the clinic, and it all kind of blurred together. So the near-miss didn’t start it, I don’t think. It just made it worse or harder to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. I wake up and there’s just this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, like before I even check the time or remember what I have to do.\n\nAnd then I’ll go to the clinic and smile at patients, or speak Spanish with someone because they look relieved when I do, and I’m trying to seem normal, but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together. It’s weird because on the outside I can still do the motions, but it doesn’t really feel like I’m okay.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever another period in your life when you felt depressed most days for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I keep trying to think back, but it’s kind of blurry. Like, there were bad weeks before, sure, but this past year feels different.\n\nMost days now I wake up and there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest before I even really do anything. And then I go to the clinic and I’m smiling at patients, sometimes speaking Spanish with them because it makes them feel more comfortable, and I look like I’m functioning, but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together.\n\nSo… before this year, I don’t know if it was ever like that for two whole weeks most days. This is the main time I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I used to have little things that helped me feel like myself, like jogging after class, or knitting—my grandma taught me this one pattern, actually, and I was trying to make a scarf but I messed up the edges and kept saying I’d fix it later.\n\nAnd I liked cooking too, or reading, just to clear my head. But now I get home and I’m still in my scrubs, sitting on my bed, and I’ll look at my running shoes or a book or whatever and I just… can’t make myself care. It feels like too much effort, even though I used to enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Even on days when work isn’t weighing on you as much, do you still feel down?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it doesn’t really seem to matter if work is lighter or not. Like, even on a slower clinic day, or if I’m not dealing with anything especially hard, I still wake up with that heavy, empty feeling in my chest.\n\nAnd I’ll still do the normal things—smile at patients, talk to them in Spanish if that helps them feel calmer—but it’s like I’m acting out being okay. Inside I just feel like I’m barely keeping it together.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has changed. I don’t really get hungry the same way, or I just don’t notice it. I’ll get busy and then suddenly it’s late in the day and I realize I haven’t eaten anything.\n\nSometimes I’ll just force down crackers or have coffee, which I know isn’t really… I mean, my grandma would yell at me for calling that a meal. She used to always make sure everyone ate, like, way too much. But lately I don’t even think about food until my stomach feels kind of hollow.\n\nAnd my work pants have been looser. I’m not trying to lose weight or anything, it just sort of… happened.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately?\n\nPatient: Not great… I mean, I get into bed and I’m exhausted, but then my brain just starts going. I’ll lie there replaying charts, like did I document that right, did I miss something, or medication calculations—just over and over.\n\nAnd then sometimes I think about how my instructor used to say you should always double-check everything, which is true, obviously, but it gets stuck in my head at night. When I finally do fall asleep, I wake up before my alarm anyway, already tired and kind of scared I forgot something important.\n\nDoctor: Since the near-miss, have you been avoiding reminders of it?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure what counts as avoiding. Like, I still go to work and do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nSometimes I kind of don’t want to talk about it, I guess, but I don’t know if that’s avoidance or just… not wanting everyone looking at me like I messed up. And the word “near-miss” makes it sound like one of those safety modules we have to click through online, where there’s a little quiz at the end and everyone just wants to finish it before lunch.\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. I guess I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a lot. I mean, “worthless” sounds so dramatic when you say it out loud, but that’s kind of what it feels like.\n\nI keep thinking that the near-miss wasn’t just a mistake, it was proof that I’m dangerous. Like maybe I’m not meant to be a nurse at all, and everyone was wrong to trust me. My aunt used to say nursing is a calling, which always made me feel weird because I don’t know if I ever felt “called,” I just wanted to help people and do something that mattered… and now I feel like I’m the kind of person people need protection from.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. I came in because I can’t stop feeling like I disappointed everyone. Like my instructors, my coworkers, my family… and patients would be better off if I just wasn’t there.\n\nDoctor: Since the near-miss, have you been feeling unusually jumpy or constantly on guard?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “constantly on guard” makes me think of like, security at the hospital entrance or those mandatory workplace violence trainings where they tell you where all the exits are.\n\nI mean, I’ve been tense, I guess, but I don’t know if it’s jumpy exactly. I don’t think I’m like… startled by every noise or anything. It’s more just hard to tell what’s normal stress right now and what isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about being dangerous feel like unwanted thoughts that keep popping into your mind, even when you try not to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Which is stupid because one of my old instructors used to say everyone makes mistakes, and we had all these checklists and simulations where they’d make you practice the same thing over and over until you wanted to scream, but… I don’t know. This feels different.\n\nI came in because I can’t stop feeling like people would be better off without me there. Like I’m the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear that you’re dangerous is probably exaggerated or unreasonable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. And then I go to the clinic and I’m smiling at patients, sometimes speaking Spanish with them because it makes them feel more comfortable, and I look like I’m functioning, but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together.\n\nSo… before this year, I don’t know if it was ever like that for two whole weeks most days. This is the main time I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Like, I don’t want to hurt anyone. That’s the whole thing, I keep thinking about how awful it would be if I did, and then I get stuck on it.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there planning something. It’s more… my mind goes back to work, and then I start thinking I shouldn’t be trusted, and then I remember random things too, like one time in school I got marked down for not double-checking something and it just feels like the same pattern, like maybe I’ve always been careless. Sorry, that’s probably not what you asked. I guess I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: When your mind goes back to the near-miss, does it ever feel as if you’re reliving it in the moment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I think about it a lot, but I don’t know if it’s like… reliving it. Sometimes I’ll suddenly remember pieces of it and feel awful, but then my mind jumps to what I should’ve done differently, or what people must think of me.\n\nIt’s hard to explain. Like yesterday I was trying to make dinner and then I remembered this tiny detail from work, and then somehow I was thinking about this teacher I had who used to say I needed to “slow down and pay attention,” which… I know isn’t exactly what you asked. I guess I don’t really know if it feels like I’m back there, or if I’m just stuck thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When you say people would be better off if you just weren’t around, have you been thinking about killing yourself?\n\nPatient: I… not like I have a plan or anything. I don’t have some way I’m going to hurt myself. It’s more that I keep having these thoughts like… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up, or if I could just disappear and then I couldn’t mess anything up again.\n\nAnd then I feel guilty even saying that, because I know people have real problems and I’m just sitting there thinking about work over and over, and then I remember stupid things I’ve done before and it kind of spirals. But no, I’m not planning to kill myself. It’s just… I don’t really want to be here sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking things or asking for reassurance because of these thoughts?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, maybe a little? Not like I have some system where I check the stove ten times or anything. I do ask people things sometimes, like if something seemed okay, but then I feel embarrassed because I don’t want to be annoying.\n\nAt work it’s harder because I keep thinking back to what happened, and then I’ll remember something unrelated, like how my mom used to tell me I’d lose my head if it wasn’t attached, and then I start thinking maybe that’s true. Sorry—checking or reassurance… I guess I haven’t really noticed it as a specific thing. I just get stuck in my head and sometimes want someone to tell me I didn’t completely ruin everything.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after the near-miss at work?\n\nPatient: Not exactly after the near-miss, no. I mean, that made everything feel louder, if that makes sense, but… this has been going on longer than that.\n\nI think it started maybe a little over a year ago—like a year and two months? After I graduated high school and started taking college classes while working full-time at the clinic. I remember thinking I should’ve been excited, but I just felt… I don’t know, weighed down. And then I’d get home and still have assignments, and my scrubs would still smell like the clinic, and it all kind of blurred together. So the near-miss didn’t start it, I don’t think. It just made it worse or harder to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. I wake up and there’s just this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, like before I even check the time or remember what I have to do.\n\nAnd then I’ll go to the clinic and smile at patients, or speak Spanish with someone because they look relieved when I do, and I’m trying to seem normal, but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together. It’s weird because on the outside I can still do the motions, but it doesn’t really feel like I’m okay.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever another period in your life when you felt depressed most days for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I keep trying to think back, but it’s kind of blurry. Like, there were bad weeks before, sure, but this past year feels different.\n\nMost days now I wake up and there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest before I even really do anything. And then I go to the clinic and I’m smiling at patients, sometimes speaking Spanish with them because it makes them feel more comfortable, and I look like I’m functioning, but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together.\n\nSo… before this year, I don’t know if it was ever like that for two whole weeks most days. This is the main time I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I used to have little things that helped me feel like myself, like jogging after class, or knitting—my grandma taught me this one pattern, actually, and I was trying to make a scarf but I messed up the edges and kept saying I’d fix it later.\n\nAnd I liked cooking too, or reading, just to clear my head. But now I get home and I’m still in my scrubs, sitting on my bed, and I’ll look at my running shoes or a book or whatever and I just… can’t make myself care. It feels like too much effort, even though I used to enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Even on days when work isn’t weighing on you as much, do you still feel down?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it doesn’t really seem to matter if work is lighter or not. Like, even on a slower clinic day, or if I’m not dealing with anything especially hard, I still wake up with that heavy, empty feeling in my chest.\n\nAnd I’ll still do the normal things—smile at patients, talk to them in Spanish if that helps them feel calmer—but it’s like I’m acting out being okay. Inside I just feel like I’m barely keeping it together.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has changed. I don’t really get hungry the same way, or I just don’t notice it. I’ll get busy and then suddenly it’s late in the day and I realize I haven’t eaten anything.\n\nSometimes I’ll just force down crackers or have coffee, which I know isn’t really… I mean, my grandma would yell at me for calling that a meal. She used to always make sure everyone ate, like, way too much. But lately I don’t even think about food until my stomach feels kind of hollow.\n\nAnd my work pants have been looser. I’m not trying to lose weight or anything, it just sort of… happened.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately?\n\nPatient: Not great… I mean, I get into bed and I’m exhausted, but then my brain just starts going. I’ll lie there replaying charts, like did I document that right, did I miss something, or medication calculations—just over and over.\n\nAnd then sometimes I think about how my instructor used to say you should always double-check everything, which is true, obviously, but it gets stuck in my head at night. When I finally do fall asleep, I wake up before my alarm anyway, already tired and kind of scared I forgot something important.\n\nDoctor: Since the near-miss, have you been avoiding reminders of it?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure what counts as avoiding. Like, I still go to work and do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nSometimes I kind of don’t want to talk about it, I guess, but I don’t know if that’s avoidance or just… not wanting everyone looking at me like I messed up. And the word “near-miss” makes it sound like one of those safety modules we have to click through online, where there’s a little quiz at the end and everyone just wants to finish it before lunch.\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. I guess I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a lot. I mean, “worthless” sounds so dramatic when you say it out loud, but that’s kind of what it feels like.\n\nI keep thinking that the near-miss wasn’t just a mistake, it was proof that I’m dangerous. Like maybe I’m not meant to be a nurse at all, and everyone was wrong to trust me. My aunt used to say nursing is a calling, which always made me feel weird because I don’t know if I ever felt “called,” I just wanted to help people and do something that mattered… and now I feel like I’m the kind of person people need protection from.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. I came in because I can’t stop feeling like I disappointed everyone. Like my instructors, my coworkers, my family… and patients would be better off if I just wasn’t there.\n\nDoctor: Since the near-miss, have you been feeling unusually jumpy or constantly on guard?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “constantly on guard” makes me think of like, security at the hospital entrance or those mandatory workplace violence trainings where they tell you where all the exits are.\n\nI mean, I’ve been tense, I guess, but I don’t know if it’s jumpy exactly. I don’t think I’m like… startled by every noise or anything. It’s more just hard to tell what’s normal stress right now and what isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about being dangerous feel like unwanted thoughts that keep popping into your mind, even when you try not to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s like I’ll try to focus on something else and then it’s just there again — that I’m dangerous, that I disappointed everyone.\n\nAnd then I start thinking maybe that one near-miss proves I’m not meant to be a nurse at all. Which is stupid because one of my old instructors used to say everyone makes mistakes, and we had all these checklists and simulations where they’d make you practice the same thing over and over until you wanted to scream, but… I don’t know. This feels different.\n\nI came in because I can’t stop feeling like people would be better off without me there. Like I’m the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear that you’re dangerous is probably exaggerated or unreasonable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. Part of me knows it sounds… too big, like one near-miss shouldn’t mean I’m just dangerous forever or that I should never be around patients again.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel exaggerated when I’m thinking it. It feels like proof that I’m a disappointment, and maybe I’m not meant to be a nurse. I keep circling back to that, like everyone would be better off if I wasn’t there. I know that’s not exactly answering it cleanly, sorry.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these depressed feelings nearly every day for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it’s been most days. I wake up and there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest before I even really do anything.\n\nAnd then I’ll go to the clinic and I can still smile at patients, like if someone needs me to explain something in Spanish I can do that, and I probably look normal enough. But inside it feels like I’m just barely keeping it together. So… yes, most days. I’m not sure I’ve counted it perfectly, but it’s been going on like that.\n\nDoctor: Outside of this past year, was there ever another time in your life when you felt this same kind of depression most days for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’ve had times before where I felt really low, like stressed with school or after a bad week, but I don’t know if it was this same thing every day for two weeks.\n\nIt’s hard to compare because back then I’d just kind of keep busy, or my mom would be asking if I ate, or I’d be doing assignments and I’d think, okay, I’m just tired. And then I’d get home and still have assignments, and my scrubs would still smell like the clinic, and it all kind of blurred together. So the near-miss didn’t start it, I don’t think. It just made it worse or harder to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. I wake up and there’s just this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, like before I even check the time or remember what I have to do.\n\nAnd then I’ll go to the clinic and smile at patients, or speak Spanish with someone because they look relieved when I do, and I’m trying to seem normal, but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together. It’s weird because on the outside I can still do the motions, but it doesn’t really feel like I’m okay.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year, was there ever another period in your life when you felt depressed most days for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I keep trying to think back, but it’s kind of blurry. Like, there were bad weeks before, sure, but this past year feels different.\n\nMost days now I wake up and there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest before I even really do anything. And then I go to the clinic and I’m smiling at patients, sometimes speaking Spanish with them because it makes them feel more comfortable, and I look like I’m functioning, but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together.\n\nSo… before this year, I don’t know if it was ever like that for two whole weeks most days. This is the main time I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I used to have little things that helped me feel like myself, like jogging after class, or knitting—my grandma taught me this one pattern, actually, and I was trying to make a scarf but I messed up the edges and kept saying I’d fix it later.\n\nAnd I liked cooking too, or reading, just to clear my head. But now I get home and I’m still in my scrubs, sitting on my bed, and I’ll look at my running shoes or a book or whatever and I just… can’t make myself care. It feels like too much effort, even though I used to enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Even on days when work isn’t weighing on you as much, do you still feel down?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it doesn’t really seem to matter if work is lighter or not. Like, even on a slower clinic day, or if I’m not dealing with anything especially hard, I still wake up with that heavy, empty feeling in my chest.\n\nAnd I’ll still do the normal things—smile at patients, talk to them in Spanish if that helps them feel calmer—but it’s like I’m acting out being okay. Inside I just feel like I’m barely keeping it together.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has changed. I don’t really get hungry the same way, or I just don’t notice it. I’ll get busy and then suddenly it’s late in the day and I realize I haven’t eaten anything.\n\nSometimes I’ll just force down crackers or have coffee, which I know isn’t really… I mean, my grandma would yell at me for calling that a meal. She used to always make sure everyone ate, like, way too much. But lately I don’t even think about food until my stomach feels kind of hollow.\n\nAnd my work pants have been looser. I’m not trying to lose weight or anything, it just sort of… happened.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately?\n\nPatient: Not great… I mean, I get into bed and I’m exhausted, but then my brain just starts going. I’ll lie there replaying charts, like did I document that right, did I miss something, or medication calculations—just over and over.\n\nAnd then sometimes I think about how my instructor used to say you should always double-check everything, which is true, obviously, but it gets stuck in my head at night. When I finally do fall asleep, I wake up before my alarm anyway, already tired and kind of scared I forgot something important.\n\nDoctor: Since the near-miss, have you been avoiding reminders of it?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure what counts as avoiding. Like, I still go to work and do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nSometimes I kind of don’t want to talk about it, I guess, but I don’t know if that’s avoidance or just… not wanting everyone looking at me like I messed up. And the word “near-miss” makes it sound like one of those safety modules we have to click through online, where there’s a little quiz at the end and everyone just wants to finish it before lunch.\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. I guess I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a lot. I mean, “worthless” sounds so dramatic when you say it out loud, but that’s kind of what it feels like.\n\nI keep thinking that the near-miss wasn’t just a mistake, it was proof that I’m dangerous. Like maybe I’m not meant to be a nurse at all, and everyone was wrong to trust me. My aunt used to say nursing is a calling, which always made me feel weird because I don’t know if I ever felt “called,” I just wanted to help people and do something that mattered… and now I feel like I’m the kind of person people need protection from.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. I came in because I can’t stop feeling like I disappointed everyone. Like my instructors, my coworkers, my family… and patients would be better off if I just wasn’t there.\n\nDoctor: Since the near-miss, have you been feeling unusually jumpy or constantly on guard?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “constantly on guard” makes me think of like, security at the hospital entrance or those mandatory workplace violence trainings where they tell you where all the exits are.\n\nI mean, I’ve been tense, I guess, but I don’t know if it’s jumpy exactly. I don’t think I’m like… startled by every noise or anything. It’s more just hard to tell what’s normal stress right now and what isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about being dangerous feel like unwanted thoughts that keep popping into your mind, even when you try not to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s like I’ll try to focus on something else and then it’s just there again — that I’m dangerous, that I disappointed everyone.\n\nAnd then I start thinking maybe that one near-miss proves I’m not meant to be a nurse at all. Which is stupid because one of my old instructors used to say everyone makes mistakes, and we had all these checklists and simulations where they’d make you practice the same thing over and over until you wanted to scream, but… I don’t know. This feels different.\n\nI came in because I can’t stop feeling like people would be better off without me there. Like I’m the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear that you’re dangerous is probably exaggerated or unreasonable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. Part of me knows it sounds… too big, like one near-miss shouldn’t mean I’m just dangerous forever or that I should never be around patients again.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel exaggerated when I’m thinking it. It feels like proof that I’m a disappointment, and maybe I’m not meant to be a nurse. I keep circling back to that, like everyone would be better off if I wasn’t there. I know that’s not exactly answering it cleanly, sorry.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these depressed feelings nearly every day for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it’s been most days. I wake up and there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest before I even really do anything.\n\nAnd then I’ll go to the clinic and I can still smile at patients, like if someone needs me to explain something in Spanish I can do that, and I probably look normal enough. But inside it feels like I’m just barely keeping it together. So… yes, most days. I’m not sure I’ve counted it perfectly, but it’s been going on like that.\n\nDoctor: Outside of this past year, was there ever another time in your life when you felt this same kind of depression most days for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’ve had times before where I felt really low, like stressed with school or after a bad week, but I don’t know if it was this same thing every day for two weeks.\n\nIt’s hard to compare because back then I’d just kind of keep busy, or my mom would be asking if I ate, or I’d be doing assignments and I’d think, okay, I’m just tired. I don’t think I kept track like that. So… maybe not? Or I just don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific stressful event or life change right before these feelings started?\n\nPatient: I think it kind of started around when everything changed after high school.
